
This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project 
to make the world's books discoverable online. 

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the public domain. A public domain book is one that was never subject 
to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books 
are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that's often difficult to discover. 

Marks, notations and other marginalia present in the original volume will appear in this file - a reminder of this book's long journey from the 
publisher to a library and finally to you. 

Usage guidelines 

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the 
public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to 
prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying. 

We also ask that you: 

+ Make non-commercial use of the files We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for 
personal, non-commercial purposes. 

+ Refrain from automated querying Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google's system: If you are conducting research on machine 
translation, optical character recognition or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the 
use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help. 

+ Maintain attribution The Google "watermark" you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find 
additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it. 

+ Keep it legal Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just 
because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other 
countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can't offer guidance on whether any specific use of 
any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book's appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner 
anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

ANCIENT AND MODERN T01tB8. 



WirKX coinpnrinjj ilii* fuiiei*t*nl atrnrtnrcs of nnricnt 
nnd nifuK'ni times, I Iiave been stniek witli one reniark- 
ahle dllierence between tlieni. Those of tlie aneients 
seem more ^^enerally to liave been bnilt for tlic ))urposo 
of exeitin;^ the sentiment of achniration, wliilc tlioso of 
tlie moderns ajipeal ratlier to onr ideas of fitness and 
])roi)riety. j\[en displayed tlicir patriotism in tlie early 
a;j;es, by consenting to give their labor for the constrnc- 
tion of some vast work that shonld proclaim their 
national greatness to the rest of the world. It was no 
niatter whether this great bnilding was devoted to the 
living or to the dead, to the pnrposes of religion or of 
war, if it was only of snlhcient magnitnde to excite the 
astonishment of mankind. The works of the ancients 
are distingnished, therefore, by their cost and magnifi- 
cence. Their temples, their palaces, and their toml)s, far 
snrpasscd the same class of strnctnres erected in modem 
times ; and the more remote the date of these ancient 
works, the more stnpendous and costly do they appear 
to be made. The inference to be drawn from these facts 
1 



2 IIOUXT AUDURN. 

is, tliat the sentiment of admiration is fully developed in 
the savage and the barbarian, and that it precedes the 
development of those finer sentiments that distinguish 
the civilized man. 

But thongh man in a barbarous state can deeply 
admire certain works and objects, his admiration must 
bo excited by something massive, stupendous, and indica- 
tive of great physical power. To arouse this feeling, 
sculpture must be colossal, architecture nuist vie with 
tlie mountains and emulate the skies, or sink deeply into 
tlio recesses of the earth. Intellect ho cannot appreciate, 
except as it is manifested by its contnil over largo masses 
of material objects. Ilenco the monarchs of ancient, 
nations sought the reverence of their subjects, not only 
by the display of mngnificcnt trophies of war, and costly 
and splendid temples and pahices, but also by the vast- 
ness and magnificence of their tombs. The sepulchres 
of Thel)es and the pyramids of Kgj'pt couhl not bo built 
in the present age. The intelligence of modern times 
would revolt at any such sacrifice of the labor of men 
for the erection of works, which could servo no better 
•purpose than to excite the awe and wonder of an 
ignorant populace. Men are not more utilitarian in 
their intellectual habits than in former times; but the 
public will not tolerate the follies of ancient destK)tisms ; 
and tho masses, being moi*o intelligent, will not submit 
to being employed in labor for which they receive no 
just recompense. The privileged classes know, that if 
they could afford to build such works, they would be- 
come a theme of ridicule and not of admiration. 

Hence, as the world has advanced in intelligence, the 
magnificence of all such structures has proportionally 
diminished. The period that preceded the invention of 
writing, or the ago of hiei*oglyphics, was tho ei>och of

sarred than by the barbarous nations, who buried their

The third epoeh was that of Christianity, when knowl- 
edge and cidtivatiou were s])read among the common 
peoj)le, through the e(|uali/,ing influence of tliis religion. 
Mankind began to think less of the particular mode of 
sepulture and more of the state of the soul after death, 
and felt, in a limited degree, the influence of those 
tender sentiments connected with the dead, which aro 
most observable in a highly civilizecl people. l>arbarous 
nations, it is true, venerate the tombs of their ancestors, 
but they manifest little of that feeling of posthumous 



4 MOUNT AUBURN. 

fricndsliip and romantic veneration for the memory of 
tlie (de[mrted which are peculiar to modern Christian 
nations. It is a sentiment too spiritualized and iK)etic 
for any of the ancients, except the Greeks and Romans. 
It seems to have come up at the same time with the 
sentiment which we call a love of nature, and is (lerhaps 
one of the legitimate fruits of Christianity. The dead 
were buried in the churchyard, from the belief that, by 
such a mode of burial, they secui*cd the salvation of the 
soul when placed under the protection of the cross ; and 
tliey were laid in a grave, that they might sleep in the 
bosom of nature, and under the sacred light of heaven. 

But Christianity has been very slow in i>eiforming its 
work. In the middle and feudal ages great expense was 
still lavished upon the tombs and monuments of kings, 
princes, and nobles. Hence the fourth epoch, or tliat of 
rural cemeteries, is of recent date, and affords an example 
probably of the most expedient and benign method of 
disposing of the remains of the dead. In the United 
States this mode of burial has been carried to the nearest 
perfection ; and though there is a proncncss among our 
people to copy some of the senseless follies of the Paris- 
ians, in manifesting their respect for the dead, and some 
of tlie useless ambition of the English, in the erection 
of costly marble monuments, — notwithstanding these 
circumstances, rural burial is probably destined to bo 
carried to perfection in beautiful simplicity, by some 
future examples in this countr}% 

When we read of the tombs of the ancients, and 
consider their grandeur and their magnitude, we ai*e 
prone to feel humiliated, because the monuments of those 
early times so far surpass our own in cost and magnifi- 
cence, and even in the genius that must have been 
required to plan and design them* But we should cease

tecture and sculi)tnre will be constantly growing less and

more comfortable and counnodions, as theli;^ht of a moro 
rational system of Christianity is shed abroad amonixnien. 
Tombs can never a^^aiu be so magnificent as in the early 
ages, \udess mankind should relapse into barbarism. j\Ien » 
will think more of nature and less of art, when providing • 
memorials and a resting place for the dead. 

The ancients built stupendous tombs, in whicli the 
dead were piled up without regard to any feeling, except 
perhaps, that of rescuing them from the dust, as if by 
end)alming and preserving their bodies, they obtained for 
them the boon of immortality. Now wc i)lacc the body 
in the grave ; wc consign dust to dust ; wc restore the 




MOUKT AUUUKN. 



remains of our fricncU to tlio bosom of the earth, and wo 
make their mortal part a liumblo oiTcnng to nature, while 
we commend the immortal spirit to the God who gave it 
The ancients had less distinct ideas of the bouVb im- 
mortality, and cherished an inferior amount of tender 
sentiment in connection ^ritli the dead. They had no 
love of nature ; since tlie love of art, for the display of 
art, precedes in the human breast, this more fervent and 
poetic feeling. Hence their tombs, — many 8i>ecimens 
of which exist on a small scale in every burial ground 
at Uie present time, — were mostly revolting objects. It 
is now customary to bury the dead in graves, covered 
with the green turf and the wild flowers of tlio field* 
Men will gradually leai*n to set less value upon art in 
this connection, and will think more of nature. They 
will learn that the only service wo ought to render the 
dead is to secure their remains from desecration in the 
grave, and to provide a simple and durable monument, 
for the record of their virtues, and to serve as the means 
of identifying their place of burial.

KURAL BURIAL. 

*' SustAincil and soothed 
Bj an unflattering trust, approach thy grave, 
Like one that wmps the drapery of his couch 
About him, and lies down to pleasant dreams.*' 



All iiiankiiid liavo nssociutcd a peculiar sacrcdncsA 
' i^'itli tlic pleaiuint scenes and objects of the natural ^vorkU 
and have indulged a ho|>e that ^vhen they died, their re- 
mains might lie dejuwited in a grave, under the jiroti^ction 
- of trees and in the bosom of nature. They love to ivtteet 
that hi death they may be Rin*roiuule<l by those ol»jects 
which were agreeable to them in their life-tinie, that the

when wc arc gone to our last rcjiose, we have no con- 
sciousness of our situation — there is sinnething witliin 
the mind which intimates that the spirit may be hovering 
near, and may even in its heavenly state feel the benign 
influence of nature that breathes around the place wliero 
its mortal dust is deposited. 

We indidgc this sentiment more deeply as it relates to

1. That it is exiKMlient to ]M)n*hase, for a Garden and

2. That a subscription be opened for lots of ground

6. That the land devoted to the i)urpose of a Cemetery 
shall contain not less than forty acres.

Horticultural Society, witliout being subject to assess- 
ments. 

8. That a Garden and Cemetery Committee of nine 
persons shall be chosen annually, first by tlie subscribers, 
and afterwards by the Horticultural Society, whoso duty 
it shall be to cause the necessary surveys and allotments 
to be made, to assign a suitable tract of land for the Gar- 
den of the Society, and to direct all matters appertaining 
to the regulation of the Garden and Cemetery ; five at 
least of this Committee shall be persons having rights in 
the Cemetery. 

9. That the establishment, including the Garden and 
Cemetery, be called by a definite name, to bo supplied 
by tlio Committee.

A friend, >vho was far from hviwjX a sentimentalist, 
once showed me, with melancholy deli;4ht, a little flower 
of the white-weed, that had blossomed upon the new- 
made grave of an infant son. He said but few words on 
tiie occasion, bnt I could perceive that he set a hi;xli 
value u|)on the flower that seemed to him, inidoubtedjy, 
like a free oU'erin;^ of nature, who, with \niseen hand, had 
reared it upon the fjrave, to memorialize the inn<»cenco 
of the child, who had been thus prematurely seized by 
death. I have no doubt that he derived more pleasure 
from this sin;;le flower than 'he would fnmi a greater 
number. Its solitary character caused it to bo more 
readily identified with the lost infant. When rambling 
in a wood, we 8to|> to admire a solitary geranium, under 
the shadow of a broad-leaved fern, while wc take no 
notice of the gaudy millions of flowers that grow in the 
open field.

There is a very simple and practicable method by

treading ui>on nature*s own ground, and we are led to

flowers, that causes it to*seem to a religious mind, tho 
intermediate ground between the busy world of man and 
tlie world of spirits ; and I am persuaded that tho charm 
of some of our old graveyards is intimately ' connected 
with this sentiment.

These remarks are not <lesi;:ned to condemn flowers as 
the ornaments of the ^rave, bnt to discourage tlieir pro- 
fusion, and to reconnnend tlie s]M)ntaneous wihlin(j;s of 
nature, nither than the careful products of art and culti- 
vation. Wild flowers arc more poetical than those of 
the ilorist, which always su;:;gest the idea of art, and of 
something; that is to bo bought and sold. Thus a wild 
rose would bo more ])leasin;^ than a rrjinlcn rose, as an 
oriuunent of a grave, because the former is a literal pro- 
duction of luiture, while the latter is lussociated with

the cultivators of the grounds, therefore, be advised care- 
fully to preserve all the sim[)le and beautiful wild flowers, 
and those conditions of the soil which are promotive of

Tills principle in our naturo that causes tho ciTect 
which is produced by a single object to be lost when a 
multitude of similar objects arc presented to the mind, 
is illustrated in tlio works of the painters as well as in 
tho works of nature. In the delineations of life thero 
must be a confinement of tlio design to one or two in- 
dividuals. Crowd together in the picture of a cottage a 
lai^ number of poor people who are so interesting in a 
single group, and tlie scene is not aflbcting. We can 
easily identify ourselves with a single family, and easily 
go along witli them in tlieir pleasures, their wants, and 
their sorrows. But we cannot sympathize with a crowd ; 
we cannot follow a multitude in their jouniey of life ; 
and if we try to do so, we soon turn away with a sense 
of confusion. Tho same may be said of historic details. 
When we read an account of tho massacre of St. Bar- 
tliolomews, when thousands were murdered at once, wo 
are struck with a confused and awful sensation of horror ; 
but our sympathies by this description are not power- 
fully excited. Indeed, I doubt if a general history of 
all the sufferings of the Polish exiles in Sil)eria would 
awaken our sympathy so i>owerfuIly, as the simple narra- 
tive of Prascovia, the daughter of the exiled Lou- 
pulofT. 

This principle, which is so carefully regarded by the 
painter and by the writer of romance, should not bo 
overlooked, when we are designing the objects of a cemo- 
tciy. The delightful, tliough melancholy sentiments, 
which are associated with a few simple flowers, from tho 
garden or the field, blooming upon the grave of a 
friend, could never be prwluced by a dazzling array of 
them in crowded profusion. Tho same remarks will 
apply to monumental sculpture. A plain headstone, 
with a single affecting device, may draw tears from hun-

I beg leave to add the account given by Virgil of ' 
funeral rites of Pallas. We find in it many ccrcmor 
iliat were used by the northern nations. Animals 
difTerent species were burned or deposited with the bo< 
The spoils of war, and wcajions of various kinds, W( 
placed on the pile; the bones and ashes were plac 
together ; and a heap of earth or a tumulus flung o^ 
tliem. Some of each of these circumstances are a 
tinually discovered in our barrows. Horns, and oti

The Tuscan chief and Trojan prince command 
To raise the funeral structures on the strand ; 
Then to the piles, as ancient rites ordain« 
Their friends convey the relics of the slain. 
From tlie black flames the sullen vapors rise. 
And smoke in curling volumes to the skies.

CURIOUS RITES OP DIFFERENT NATIONS. 

Museovian Funerals. — In JMuscovy, when a man cl 
liis friends and relations iinmedintely assemble, nnd s 
themselves in a cirele around tlic corpse, nnd nsk 
dead the following questions: — ** Why have you die 
Is it because your commercial ntlairs went bndl 
Or was it because you could not obtain the acco 
plbliment of your desires? Was your wife deficit 
in youth and beauty? Or has she been fiiithless 
her obligations?** They then rise nnd quit the hou 
When they carry the body to be buried, it is cover 
and conveyed on a bier to the brink of the intend 
grave ; the covering is then withdrawn, the priest ivf 
some prayers, the company kiss the dead, and reti 
These ceremonies finished, tlie priest places between 1 
fingers of the dead man a piece of ]>nper, signed by 1

Christian. This, they suppose, serves as a passport to 1

deceased, St. Peter, when he sees it, will open to h 
• the gates of eternal life. The letter given, tlie corj 
is removed, and placed in the gmve, with the face 
wards the east. 

Interesting Custom. — A custom that was once pre^ 
lent in'Simin is deserving of notice. When any o 
dies, the relations, friends, and ne!ghboi*s, carry to t 
survivors, at meal times, for three days, one or mc 
plates of food, under tito idea that the grief tliey fi 
will not allow them to think on nutriment. Some p< 
ons also accomjMiny these dishes, in order to ofier co 
solation to the family.

MOUNT AUBURN.

Hers I will rest, upon this Iiillsidc fniri 

And muso upon the scenes within these grounds, 

Where towering oaks kce]) out the mid-dny glare, 

From whose bnmd toim come forth sweet mellow sounds^ 

Like funeral chants o*er these sqmlchral mounds* 

I am alone, and I would wish it so ; 

For with high interest the si)ot abounds, 

And while my thoughts with solemn fervor glow, 

I would a lesson learn, ere to the world I go. 

It is tlie hush of Autifnm's solemn tide ; 

Far in the west the Sun lijs course hath spent ; 

Across the heavens the wild clouds swiftly ride, 

While scarce a ray to light my path is lent: 

*T is true I come no lost friend to lament, 

Yet I Ve a tear to give to those who mourn ; 

And even now my rising sighs are spent. 

As towards yon grave with musing steps I turn, 

Where virtue lies enshrined beneath the voiceless urn. * 

I love the spot, for bright in memory^s page. 
Comes up the day, when bidding books farewell, 
With buoyant steps I came to hear the sago 
Whose silver voice arose from yonder dell, • 
While crowds sat breathless as his accents fell.

It was a lovely day, tlio moriung sun 

Wnlked in ricli splumlor up the ambient sky,

Eacli haunt of t\m fair wood glowed with his brilliuncjr* 

But all, how cliangetl ! tlu!* lovely spat tlien seemed

And ieel its lagging powers agahi in lilb revivop 

The evening shades arc fast assembling round, 

And to his airy seat each songster I lies, 

While all is hushed throughout this haHowed ground, 

Save where from yonder mart low sounds arise, 

That lull the ear like gentle melodies. 

And now I bid these scenes a sad farewell. 

Where many a noble breast in quiet lies : 

Ere I again shall come, ah I who can tell 

How many a breathing form may seek its narrow cell.

Bcpiilclinil works. Figures representing love, or pity, or

All who have gazed u|)on the jilain coihmI stone coffin, 
or those ornamented by a cross, sometimes of the most

manner. 

liy the laws of Solon, no one was permitted to raise a

than three dap in its construction. The Council of

chral monuments; and Philip II. of Si>ain, in 15G5, 
directed that monuments should not 1)0 erected in

THE SErULCIIRES AT TIIEBE3, 



u 



Tub sepulchre,') of Memphis were placed in splendid 
pyramids, wholly above ground^ and built upon a rock ; 
hut at Theljea they .ire subterranean entirely. They aro 
situated in a spot about a mile from the walla of the city, 
whieh is, from this appropriation, called the ** Valley of 
Death/* I'a^ing up a narrow ravine, barren ami dcso^ 
late, in which not a blade of verdure is to be «cen, tlio 
visitor beholds on the left liand the apertures that lead to 
the tombs. When I was there^ there were twenty-four 
of them open ; but since then, Bclzoni lias discovered 
10 



no MOUNT AUDURN. 

twen^ more. There may very probably be one hundred 
more yet to bo developed by tlio researches of future 
travellers. Sucli of them as are accessible, resemble each 
other so closely in all respects, tliat a description of one 
may serve for tlie whole. 

The excavations are carried tlirough the solid rock, 
deep into tlie bowels of tlio mountain. Some of these 
rocks are of limestone, others a species of sandstone. Tlie 
entrance of tlio sepulchre is an aperture of twenty by 
twelve or fifteen feet This leads to a perforation or 
tunnel, tlio walls of which are made to incline inward for 
the sake of greater strength, and which extends in none 
of them less than one, and in some as much as two miles. 
At tlie termination of this tunnel wo come to the Hall of 
Death. It is wider than tlio passage leading to it, and 
contains an altar and a sarcophagus. In some this latter 
is made of sienito granite ; in others of basnlt ; in others 
of a species of green basalt ; and in others again, the 
sarcophagus is of alabaster. Belzoni brought one of these 
away, and carried it to London. 

These extensive jierforations through solid rock must 
liave cost an amazing amount of labor. Each of tlicm 
is equal in size to the far-famed tunnel under the I'hames, 
the cost of which was nearly three millions of dollars. 
Admitting that the multitudes employed in these excava- 
tions were slaves, and wrought without wages, still they 
must have been clotlied and fed, so that tlio cost must 
have been very great. But the mere hewing and re- 
moval of the stone was but half the task. As the rock 
left a rough surface, the whole had to be covered over 
with stucco, an inch and a half or two inches in depth, 
like the composition called chunam^ employed for artificial 
hearths or floors. On tliis substance were sculptured 
battles, triumphs, sacrifices, and all tlioso representations.

religious emotions, to which the burial place ought always 
to minister. We stroll among tho most interesting of 
ruins, and while we look upon the old headstones, with 
their quaint emblems and devices, our reverence for tho 
dead is exalted to a quiet enthusiasm, and all our thoughts 
are raised to heaven. 

There issomo danger that the custom of building 
showy monuments in our modem cemeteries may prevent 
our posterity from visiting them with tho same feelings 
which wo ex|)erience when contemplating those of our 
predecessors. The simplicity that pervades theso old 
grounds is absent from tho new, which are filled with 
exhibitions of living pride. Time, it is true, will cast a

tliote feelings of reverence for their ancestors which the 
scenes inspire. Men who have but little reflection or 
sensibility, are by these objects converted for a time into 
poets and philosophers* The skeptic cannot enter here, 
without a temporary revival of those charming religious 
influences that filled and inspired his youthful mind* 
Above all, do persons of religious feeling and poetic imag- 
ination love to ponder among these scenes* Nature and 
art and religion have imitcd to form hero a combination 
of objects tending to humanixo those who have forgotten 
their love for their fellow-beings, and to mise the tlioughts 
of the virtuous still further above the world. 

Notwithstanding the vcnernblo and interesting charac- 
ter of these old cemeteries, there is a disixwition among 
those who would sweep away everything with the besom 
of •• improvement," to remove them, to make room for 
land speculations* Every old graveyard, though right 
in the midst of a village, should be preserved, not to bo 
used for new graves, but set apai*t as hallowed ground* 
The trees that stand there have formed a grove, which 
ought to be as sacred as any that were in ancient days 
consecrated to philosophy. Let them never bo disturbed, 
that tlie ground may be used for ordinary purposes. Let 
tliem be cherished as memorials of the simple habits of ^ 
our ancestors, who, though often condemned for their 
disregard for tlie ideal and the beautiful, never wandei*ed 
in search of the vanities that characteri/x; the present 
times. It is hardly probable that we shall escape the 
satire of posterity, as our predecessors have escaped our 
own* Though no living mourners are seen to weep in 
these grounds, which have long ceased to be a place of 
sorrow ; yet they who go there, while pondering among 
the graves of their ancestors, may be persuaded to imitate 
their unostentatious virtues, and learn that wisdom which 
may be read in tlieir humble biography.

Ouu vales arc sweet with fern and rose, 
Our hills are maple crowned ; 

lUit not from them our fathers choso 
The village burying-ground. 

The dreariest spot in all tlic land 

To Death they set apart ; 
With scanty grace from Nature's hand, 

And none from that of Art. 




MOUNT AUBURV. 

A winding wall of mosAjr stone. 

Frost-flung and broken, lines 
A lonesome acre^ thinly grown 

With grass and wandering vines* 

Without tlio wall a birch-tree shows 

Its drooped and tasscUcd head ; 
Within, a stag-homed sumach grows, 

Fem-Icafod with spikes of red. 

There sheep that graze the neighboring plun, 
Like white ghosts come and go ; 

The fiirm-horso drags his fetlock chain, — 
The cow-boll tinkles slow. 



? I 



, I 1 



Low moans the river from its bed. 

The distant pines reply ; 
Like mourners shrinking from the dead. 

They stand apart and sigli. 

Unshaded smites the summer sun, 

Unchecked the winter blast ; 
The school-girl learns the place to shun, 

With glances backward cast. 

For thus our fathers testified — - 
That he might read who ran — 

The emptiness of human pride. 
The nothingness of man. 

They dared not plant the grave with flowers. 

Nor dress the ftmeral sod, 
Where with a love as deep as ours. 

They left their dead with God.

Above the graves the blackherry hung 
In hloom mid preen its wreath, 

And hare-belli swung as if they rung 
The ell hues of peace l>etieath* 

Tlic beauty Nature loves to share, 

The gifts she Imth for all, 
The common light, the common air, 

0\»rerept the graveyard's wall. 

It knew the glow of eventide, 

The sunrise and tlic noon, 
And glorified and sanctified,

With flowers or snowflakes for Its sod. 

Around the seasons ran, 
And evermore the love of God 
, Rebuked the fear of man. 

We dwell with fears on cither hand, 

Within a daily strife. 
And spectral problems waiting stand. 

Before the gates of life.

Tlie doubts we vainly seek to solve. 
The truths we know are one ; 

Tlie known and nameless stars revolve 
Around the Central Sun. 

And if we reap as we have sown, 

And take the dole we deal, 
The law of pain is love alone. 

The wounding is to heal. 

Unharmed from change to change we glide. 

We fall as in our dreams ; 
The far-off terror at our side, 

A smiling angel seems. 

Secure on God*s all tender heart. 

Alike rest great and small ; 
Why fear to lose our littlo part, 

When he is pledged for all ? 

O fearful heart and troubled brain I 
Take hope and strength from this, -^* 

That Nature never hints in vain, 
Nor prophesies amiss. 

Her wild birds sing the same sweet stave, 
Her lights and aii*s are given, 

Alike to play-ground and the grave, — 
And over both is Heaven.

Tlie Egyptians exhausted all their skill and science in

for tliough, as recent experiment has proved,

This custom of embalming originated, perhaps, in the 
opinion which we are told they held, that so long as a 
body remained uncomipted, so long the soul continued

At a Scythian king's funeral, the moumers disfigured

Barrows, or hninensc mounds of earth, are supposed to 
bo the most ancient and the most general scpulchml 
monuments in the world. They are fiiund in almost 
every part of the habitable glolw, having been pixjscr^xd,

human kind ; but, indeed, the contents of these tumuli 
are as varied as are the habits of tlio diflfercnt people 
who occupy the world. 

Dr. Clarke, speaking of the barrows in Russia, says : — 
'* Throughout the whole of this country are seen, dis- 
I)ersed over immense plains, mounds of earth covered 
with fine turf, the sepulchres of the ancient world, com- 
mon to almost every habitable country. If there exist 
anything of former times which may afford monuments 
of antediluvian manners, it is this mode of burial." 

In the Now World barrows are the inseparable ajv 
pendages to great settlements. They are of various forms, 
proportions and sizes. They are called Indian graves ; 
and one in Virginia was opened which contained the 
bones of nearly one hundred persons. 

This mode of burial was gmdually discontinued in 
every country, as civilization inci*eased and refinement 
advanced. The ban*ows mised over the remains of

In Pagan rites by night, torches were necessarily

Instead of hired mourners* shrilly, keening, dismal

and chnstened it may be, by tears, did yet assume some- 
what of triumphant aspect, which relieve<l those most 
closely connected, most serenely bereaved, from some of 
the bitterest feelings of separation. 

And these solemn ofterings of prayer and praise were 
invariably accompanied by alms-giving; the poor and 
needy were always remembered. This was the origin 
of those *' Funeral Doles " which afterwanls became a 
component part of a re»]x;ctable person's funeral. 

From the time that Constantine ascended the throne, 
the Christians had free privilege to inter their dead, and 
they performed these rites in the open day. Before this 
time, it was a refinement. in cruelty with their persecu- 
tors, to interfere with the sacred duty of burial.

When a bishop died, it was usual to carry his coq^so

resting place. The body was usually laid on a bed of

The poor were buried in coffins of plain wood, at the 
common charge of the church ; but this duty was not 
left to indiscriminate care. Early in the fourth century, 
two classes were instituted, whose specific vocation was 
to solace the sick, and |>ay duo and requisite attention to 
the dead. The one were called jyarabolani^ from tlio 
venturing their lives among the sick in contagious dis- 
, orders ; the other, copiata:^ laborantesy lecticarii^ fossarii^ 
and decaniy whose office was to dig graves for the poor, 
carry the coffins, deposit them in the ground, &c., as 
most of the names signify. 

These officers, kept then under the rigid discipline and 
surveillance of the church, are the progcnitoi^s of the 
fruitful progeny of undertakers, sextons, &c., who in these 
days cause many a heart-broken person to count with 
despair, the few coins in a purse, which, perhaps, has 
been impoverished by the hand of God himself, in heavy, 
long, lingering sickness.

Nono was denied this privilege of psalmody nt the 
funeral, except suicides, or criminals who were publicly 
executed, or those who died in the wilful neglect of holy 
baptism. 

If the life of the departed, or his cliaracter, had been 
marked by any circumstances available as example to 
others, some few wonis woro spoken as a just memorial 
of his merit, and with reference to him as a pattern to 
those around. Several of those funeral orations, made 
in tlie early ages of Christianity, are still extant 

If the interment were in the forenoon, the whole ser- 
vice of the church was gone through, and the Holy 
Eucharist was administered ; if it were in the afternoon, 
the psalmody and prayers only, accompanied by the more 
especial funeral service. 

This service consisted of hymns of thanksgiving for the 
deceased, with prayer for one entering into that eternal 
rest. The bishop gave solemn thanks to God, for his 
(the departed's) i)ei*sovcrance in tlie knowledge of God, 
and in his Christian warfare even unto death ; and the 
deacon read such portions of Scripture as contained the 
promises of the resurrection. A hymn to the same pup- 
pose was sung. 

During the celebration of the Holy Communion in 
those dap, a solemn commemoration was made of the 
dead in general, and prayers were offered to the Al- 
mighty for them. And this was one especial reason for 
the adoption of this service at burials, because prayers 
were constantly made therein for all holy men and holy 
women departed, among whom was especially named 
him about to be committed to the grave. 

The kiss of peace is s|x>ken of, and the anointing with 
holy oil, as the last rites of all ; but these seem not to 
have been always observed. It was very usual to strew 







flowers on tho grave ; and no old writer, how ri;;id 
Bf>cvcr» has reprobated i\m innocent, Wautiful, and most 
in^Xfjcstive custom. 

And so fnUillcd with tlio prnco and benediction of 
Him whom tlicy lind leamt^d to know of their Father ia 
Heaven, nn their Ilctleeiner to all eternity, in faitli and 
hope, in tho exerrise of prayer and almsgiving, tho early 
Chiistmns wero onahled to givo hearty thanki to God, 
that ho had heon plenaed to '* deliver tlicir brethren out 
of the nilserlos of this sinful world/'

EPITAPHS AND IHSCRIPTIONS. 



Every person of intelligence and sensibility la alive to 
tlic beauties of a. brief, simple, and appropriate epitapli 
wliicli excites a reverence for the dead, and awakens an 
interest in the evcnt3 of hh life* Wben we encounter a 
headstone vvitliout an epitaphH, it seems like a book with a 
mere title page, while the leaves that fallow aro blank. It 
is oil indispensable appendage to a monument, and w^e 
turn from one that is without it as from a w^m*k of sculp- 
ture that is unfinished* Tlie propriety of this tribute to 
the dead is universally admitted ; and it is not, therefor^?, 
a useless task to endeavor to define the princi[>les by 
which the composition of it Bhould ho governed ; for if 
one that is appropriate and well written, is pleasing to the 
most indifferent reader, one that is awkward, liigh* 
sounding or exaggerated, is ludicrous and demeaning to 
the character of the subject. 

There are some epitaphs that relate particulai'ly to tho 
dead, and are commonly panegyrical ; others that make 
no direct allusion to the dead, but aim merely to convoy 
n ])]easing sentiment or an instructive moral. Tho 
former ai'o the most difhcult work for the writer ; be- 
cause it requires great discrimination, in elegiac compo* 
sition, to avoid the extreme of panegyric, or to present, 
in a few words, tho most appropriate thoughts and images, 
and to select those points which would produce tho most 
vivid effect upon the mind of tho reader. If one is extrav- 
ogant in his praises of tho dead, tho reader is sceptical of 
the truth of those praises ; if tho epitaph be long, it will 
not bo read ; and though it were brief, the points selected 
may not be those which would produce the most favor* 
ableimpression.

It has been tlio custom ninong writers of epitaphs to 
aim at antithesis; to express ]K>inted thoughts in ap- 
posite words and plirases. This is the surest method of 
clothing a commonplace thouglit, or a trite image, with 
the appearance of originality ; but this style of writing 
is too artificial to seem to flow from tlie heart. When 
the composition is evidently studied, it loses its charm 
for the reader ; though he may know at the same time, 
that the most pleasing simplicity is often the result of 
great art and elaboration. When one reflects that the 
living friend could write nothing of the deceased except 
a pointed epigram, he is prone to imagine that, as there 
was nothing in his character to be praised, he was com- 
memorated only by a witticism. Sincere praise is often 
exaggerated, but never pointed and rhetorical. 

" The difficulty," says Dr. Johnson, ** in writing epi- 
taphs, is to give a particular and appropriate praise. 
This, however, is not always to be performed ; for the 
greater part of mankind have little that distinguishes 
theni from others equally good or bad ; and, therefore, 
nothing can be said of them, which may not, with equal 
propriety, be applied to a thousand more. *It is, indeed, 
no great panegyric, that there is inclosed in this tomb, 
one who was bom in one year, and who died in another ; 
yet many useful and amiable lives have been spent, which 
leave little materials for any other memorial. These, 
however, are not the proper subjects of poetry ; and 
whenever friendship or any other motive obliges a poet 
to write on such a subject, he must be forgiven if he 
sometimes wanders in generalities, and utters the same 
praises over different tombs. 

The scantiness of human praises can scarcely be made 
more apparent, than by remarking how often Pope has, 
in the few epitaphs which he composed, found it neces-

" He lived in peace, because he was just ; " 

" Jle died in liope, because ho was a Cliristiau/' 

These lines convey no fulsome panegyric, and yet no

wonls. 'They contain a two-fold moral, showing tho

belief in Christianity, to die with a liopc of Heaven. 

A part of the difHculty, attending tho composition of 
epitaphs, arises from the effort of tlio writer, to express 
ideas and images which are not obvious, without con- 
sidering that this effort, if it be kpivirent, spoils all their 



164 ifouirr aububn. 

effect. It 18 not the highest praise that is most exaggcrar 
ted, for high praise expressed in plain and simple terms, 
if it produces conviction in the mind of tlie reader, gives 
rise to no invidious feelings. A smaller amount of eulogy 
conveyed in high-sounding language, betrays the wish of 
the writer to raise his subject to an undue importance, 
and fails in producing conviction, because it excites our 
incredulity. An epitaph should always contain more 
eulogy than is apparent, like a strong and even light that 
illuminates a room without dazzling tlie eyes. 

It has been customary, at certain times, to omit any 
inscription upon tlie tomb, except the name and age of 
the deceased, and perhaps some other indispensable rec- 
ords. This neglect probably originated in a conscious- 
ness of the abuse which has been made of epitaphs ; their 
extravagance in some instances, and their triteness or 
absurdity in others. These evils were thought to be 
avoided by omitting the epitaph entirely. But if one 
objects to the panegyrical epitaph, he might use the 
other form, in which a sentiment or a moral is merely 
recorded upon the tomb, without particular mention of 
the character or history of the subject. Such is that 
common, but most appropriate and delightful sentiment, 
which has lost nothing by repetition, and is often in- 
scribed upon the tombstono of a little child : 

*• Not lost, but gone before.*' 

There is still another form of epitaph in which the 
person commemorated is represented as speaking. Tlicso 
different forms of inscription seem to give variety to the 
expression of the same ideas ; and as one fonn is not 
absolutely preferable to another, the writer should be 
governed in his choice by his own taste. Of the last

The themes which, by general consent, are regarded as 
the proper subjects for an epitaph, are the virtues and 
good actions of the deceased ; the lessons which his life 
and death may impart to the living ; the hoi>es ho enter- 
tained of hai)i)incss beyond the grave ; rest from the toils 
and cares of this world ; the soul's immortality and en- 
trance into a new life. These are the appropriate sub-

• 

jects of diflcoune in monumental inscriptions; and there 
seems to be no good reason for rejecting them, on account 
of the difficulty of avoiding tlie repetition of ideas which 
have been recorded many times before. Tlie same ob- 
jection might bo made to the erection of a headstone over 
tlie grave of a friend, because a new pattern could not bo 
invented. We must not expect the works of art to ex- 
ceed the variety of nature. The forms of trees are 
but the repetitions of resemblances ; but tlie landscape is 
not rendered tiresome by their similarity. Neitlier is a 
cemeteiy necessarily tiresome on account of the frequent 
recurrence of similar monumental stones. 

The visitor is not expected to read tho inscriptions 
upon all tlie gravestones. He may read many before he 
meets with a literal repetition of a previous one. If tho 
words, ^ Not lost, but gone before " were inscribed on 
fifty stones in Mount Auburn, a stranger who should 
linger an hour in these grounds might not see them but 
once. As we cannot invent anything now, wo must 
be satisfied with presenting an oiV-repeatcd thought in a 
new phase, or by making a new application of it. It 
would be as unwise to leave the stone without nn epitaph, 
on account of Uie difficulty of saying a new thing, as to 
refuse tliis tribute to a friend, because his virtues were 
not brilliant, but of tho humble sort, that do not seem to 
elicit eulogy. These are, indeed, the virtues which are 
the most appropriate themes for monumental inscription. 
^^ it is better to dwell on those general traits of humanity 
which are common to all good men, than to confine tho 
epitaph to certain extraordinary qualities. We do not 
come to the grave to study and analyze each person's 
peculiarities of character. We are better pleased with a 
few words, expressing in general terms his virtuous and 
peaceful life, and its happy and hopeful termination, than

An epitaph is of no value, if it does not obtain tho 
faith and the symimthy of tho general reader. For this 
end it should givo proof of the writer's own deep feeling 
and sincerity. He must address tho reader, therefore, as 
a humble friend of the departed, and not as a sermonist or 
a censor. He must be serious and solemn, but not 
gloomy; believing and hoi)eful, but not extravagantly 
elated. Ilis lamentations must ho heartfelt, but not too 
painfully wrought; for the reader, though he loves to 
sympnthixe, does not wish to l>e afflicted. We sympathisco 
more easily with sorrow that is sincere without despon- 
dency ; for we wish to sec a probability that tho mourner 
will obtain relief and a renewal ofhappiness^'as wcare do- 
lighted with the promise of morning that gleams through 
the darkness of night. An epitaph should make no pa- 
rade of one's lamentations, any more than of the virtues 
of tho subject. As a silent tear flowing down tho check 
of an unqucstionablo mourner, excites moro sympathy 
than boisterous wailing, so does one lino of tender an- 
guish affect the sensibility of the reader more deeply than 
a long paragraph of earnest com])laInt. 

A sepulchral monument is no placo for wit or for 
satire. We may bo excited to mirth by a humorous 
epitoph upon a gi*avestone ; but it interrupts the flow of

tender melancholy which one is disposed to cherish in his 
meditations among the tombs. It disqualifies the mind 
to receive congenial impressions, and docs not avert 
gloomy reflections with the same power as the ho]>criiI 
utterance of religious faith. Satire, which is always 
• more or less malignant, ought to find no place here. 
Anything like malice or contempt towaixls our fellow 
beings, should never be exhibited in these sacred inclos- 
nrcs. The sight of the graves of our fellow men brings 
forcibly to mind the reflection that we arc all travelling 
tlio same road ; and here we should unite in mutual 
trust and forbearance ; and if we have lessons to impart 
to the living in the lines which we carve upon the monu- 
ments of the dead, let them bo conveyed in the simple 
language of love. I/ct the graveyard bo a school of re- 
ligion and virtue, not a place for the wit of the epigram- 
taiatist or the sneers of the misanthroiie. 

Deatli must be mentioned as our inevitable fate, and as 
the occasion of sorrow ; but not as the cause of despon- 
dency, or the destroyer of ho|X!. The tomb should be 
invested with those circumstances that will shed light on 
the gloom of the grave ; and nothing serves more eflec- 
tually to difliise this cheerfulness around it, than a 
poetical and hopeful inscription that points to a world 
beyond this mortal sphere. 

The individual commemorated is to bo presented to the 
reader as one who has not lived in vain, nor died with- 
out hope ; and the claims upon the reader's interest and 
sympathy should be based on his ordinary, not extraor- 
dinary deserts. The first idea commands our sympathy, 
the second excites our incredulity. If tlie subject has 
perfonned certain noble and heroic acts, it is better to 
•name the acts, and let them praise him, than to follow

tlie temple or the statue with appropriate adilressi appear

tliat no species of composition, when well written, can 
better answer the wishes of the friends of virtue than 
this ; and almost every iHilishcfl nation, tlieivfore, has 
. made use of it to impress the feeling nu'ntl and incite it

success, and the Anthology abounds in ]>icces of this

It will not be an employment altogether void of in- 
terest, perhaps, to trace and give a f(*w s|K*ciniens of these

place, than tho attempt to decorate in this manner. 
Should tho inscription bo ill-chosen, or the scene ill-

tempt or disgust will inevitably follow, and the disn]!- 
pointed contriver, become an object of ridicule. Tho

Some of Hcrrick's prettiest lines run thus : — * 

•* Follow me weeping to my turf, cind tliero 
Let fall a primrose, and witli it a tear ;

Rosemary, so commonly used' at weddings, is in great 
request at funerals, in several parts of England, even 
to this day. In former times it was considered indispen- 
sable. Friar Lawrence, when the Capulets are weeping 
over Juliet, directs thus : — 

•* Stick your rosemary 
On this fair corse ; and as the custom is. 
In all her best array, bear her to cluux;h."

** Upon her grave the rosemary thoy thi'ew,

Herrick's couplet shows its constant adaptation to

Be it for my bridal or my burial."

a sprig.

ally employed to convey to the mind an image of the 
soul's coiulition in the future world, and to impress it 
with a belief of things which are only dimly seen by Ihe

An emblem may be defined a visible image, or a pictui*e 
that suggests to the mind the idea of some abstract tnith. 
It is indeed a ])ictuivd allegoiy or parable. In the East, 
emblems ai*e still freely used in profane as well as in sacred 
things. Among the Pei'sians (ire is the emblem of their 
Deity, expivssing by the same image his power and his 
beneficence — heat being the source of all life, and having 
power to destroy^ all created fonns of matter. The ser- 
pent with its tail in its mouth is an Eg}'ptian emblem of 
eternity ; but modem taste revolts at its puerility, and it 
really affords no idea of infinity, such as might be repre* 



188 MOUMT AUBURIf. 

scnted bj a light ni^hose rays extend illiniitaUy in all 
directions from the centre. There is a savor of tho 
burlesque in this emblem of the serpent ; and it is sur- 
prising that any person will admit it among tho devices 
of monumental sculpture. 

In India there are still in existence statues of immense 
si»s which arc emblematical of virtue. They nro furnish- 
ed with several arms, to indicate the necessity of so many 
forces to enable one to contend successfully ngninst vice. 
Xenophanes, an ancient poet, remarks in certain verses, 
that every animal suggests images that assist the human 
mind in forming conceptions of the Deity. The wings of 
birds are associated with the idea of progress upward and 
through space, without contact with the earth. Hence 
they are found in all mythological pictures of supernatural 
beings, and are not confined to tlie Jewish and Chris- 
tian theology. Many of the Pagan Deities are furnished 
with wings which, in the sacred books, are confined to 
angels. The image of the true God needs no such aid, 
as he is everywhere present at the same moment. By 
the Hebrew Prophets he is represented as seateil on the 
clouds, from which he issues his commands to the inhabi- 
tants of the earth. There is no material image that 
aifoixls so exalted an idea of the power of tlie Deity as 
this. Plato, and after him Pascal, in his ^' Pensees,*' 
adopted the beautiful emblem of Timceus of Locris, who 
describes the Deity by the image of '' a circle whose 
centre is ever}'where, and whose circumference no- 
where." 

One of the most delightful of our sacivd emblems is 
that which represents Hope as the image of a female 
leaning upon an anchor, the symbolical representation of 
steadfastness and confidence, without which hope cannot 
exist This is a very appropriate emblem for a cemetery,

Tho emblDm of the cross is. historical, referring to tho 
manner of the death of Jesus Christ, and is intended to

blematical of tho Christian religion. Tho drooping figure

mother and child in the same grave, and the image of a 
lamb alone is emblematical of an infant. A butterfly 
just emerged from a chrysalis is intended to represent the 
mortal putting on immortality.

Hardly more diversified ar© tl»e nations who peopU 
ilia earth, than are the customs and obscr%*iinces uaed by 
til em to Bigtmll^ the arrival of the cotnmoncat of nil 
visitors^ though most awful of all guests, the ** black 
veiled khig of the dead." The Jews of old rent their 
garments and .sprinkled dust on their heads, a practice 
followed Uk this day in Abyssinia. The practice of 
tearing tho ganncnts isj wo are told, eommutod by tlie 
Jews of these economical days into cnrefully cutting 
ft way a small, and probably a perfectly insignificant por« 
tion thereof. Tlicy bottled their tears also, a custom 
referred to in tlio 5Gth Psalm; and that this practice was 
customary with tho Greeks and Romans, the number of 
lachrymatories, or tear bottler, found among their sepul- 
chral remains, sufficiently testifies* 

A late writer has |>ojnted out tho analogy between a 
mourning custom of tho Australian savages of toKlay, 
and of the ancient Hebrews, vi/^, the cutting or scratch- 
ing the face with tho nails, tearing the flesh between the 
eyes, and otherwise maiming the person, as is tho custom 
of the female aborigines of Austmlia on the death of a 
relativei Hence tho warning in Holy Writ — ** Ye 
diall not" make any cutting in your flesh for the dead, 
nor print any marks upon you/* I#ev. xix. 28, 

The " cup of consolation " referred to in Scripture, 
and tho '^ bread of mourning,** sometimes called also the 
*' bread of bitterness /* were the refreshments always 
among tlie Jews, supplied by friends to the bereaved 
person on bis return from the funeral — in its origin a ' 
most kind and hospitable relief to the bereaved family. 



200 MOUNT AUBURN. 

The Jews chcrislicd their grief in every way ; tliey in- 
vited it ; they pampered it ; they took all pains to recall 
the poignancy of their affliction. Tliey ate tlieir food 
seated on tlie ground and without shoes. For three days 
tliey strove not to repress their tears. For seven days 
people came morning and evening to weep \^ith them. 
At the end of seven days the mourner might attend the 
synagogue ; but thirty days must elajMO ere he was al- 
lowed to bathCf or to dress his beard. 

In many countries the term of mourning was fixed by 
law. The Jews, as we have seen, mourned thirty days ; 
the Lacedo^imonians but eleven ; the Eg}'ptian8 from forty 
to seventy days. Romulus fixed a widow's mourning at 
ten months, tlio length of his year. The Imperial Code not 
only ordained a year's mourning, but declared the widow 
infamous if she married within that period. Tlie time of 
mourning is fixed by law in China, tliree years being the 
period required for a parent. 

The Jewisli fashion of throwing ashes on the head, 
beating the breast and tearing the flesh with the nails, 
was, on occasions of peculiar concernment, adopted by the 
Greeks. But in addition to the funeral feasts, which 
among Greeks and Uomans soon ceased to wear an entirely 
lugubrious asjicct, they enlivened their moUuicholy with 
games and funeral processions. These entci*tainmcnts 
among the Greeks consisted chiefly of horse-races, where 
garlands of parsley were awarded to the victors. The 
Roman games were processions, and the very character- 
istic entertainment of the mortal strife of gladiators and 
the funeral pile. These funeral games were abolished by 
the Emperor Claudius.

of cutting off the hair and casting it on the body or into 
the tomb. So did the Roman women on Virginia; so

*' The lamentation is not alwap confined to the keener.

From the old classical cpitliot of ** black veiled king of 
the ^dcad/' one would suppose that black had been uni- 
versally, as with ourselves, the mouniing color. Not 
so, however. Plutarch writes tliat in their mourning, 
women laid aside their purple, gold and jewelry, and 
clothed themselves in white, ** like as then the dead body 
was wrapped in white clothes. This color was thought 
the fittest, because it is clear, pure, and sincere, and least 
defiled.'' 

So in our own country (Engliind) some white, an em- 
blem of purity, is alwap displayed on the hearse and 
pall of a child or unmarried person. In the northern 
parts of England, indeed, a white linen scarf or hat^band 
is an indispensable part of mourning for the d^ad at any 
age. 

Coarse red hempen cloth is the only dress allowed in 
China for the first and deepest mourning. In time this 
is changed to white ; and silk may bo worn in half- 
mourning, but blue or white sleeves are indispensable. 
White being chosen as expressive of the belief that the 
dead are in heaven, the place of purity. So more pnu> 
tically in Egypt yellow is chosen, because it represents 
natural decay as exhibited in fruits and flowers ; whilst 
in Turkey blue is often adopted to denote the sky as the 
place of departed spirits. All this, however, whether in 
good or bad taste, is moveable mourning; but we are 
told that the first duty of the women of Medina, on as- 
suming mourning, is to dyt the hands witli indigo.

robed mutes unci weepers. It is wr(mg that this pomp

The mourning which Christ hath hallowed — for he 
wept far Lazams — has no communion with crai)e bands 
and weepers. There is no teacher like Death. In his 
dread presence tfio great mystery of life o|>ens on the 
sorrowing heart, the awakened mind. lie teaches that 
faith and hope by which tlie biiiised seed is bound, the 
broken heart healed ; and as fragrance, which in its peiv 
fectness was unknown, emanates from an herb when it is 
crushed, so does sorrow develope virtues and consolations 
undreamt of in gay and happy hours. 

Thus does the faithful mourner Icam that sorrow and 
pain and suifering — those *^ many waters," which threats 
ened but did not overwhelm — passed, the purified and 
renewed spirit will emerge on that happier shore, where 
. sin and sorrow are unknown, where tears are wiped from 
every eye, and where the toil-worn, grief-worn, stricken, 
but contrite denizen of the earth, shall stand blessed, pure 
and happy as a little child, in the presence of his Creator. 

And so chastened and subdued, and passing *' cheerly 
on through prayer unto the tomb," tlie true mourner 
looks beyond that solemn vestibule, to re-union with those 
deeply and endoringly loved on earth, who are — not 
lost — but gone before.

GRAVES WITHOUT A STONE. 

Not every good, nor eveiy great man, lins had a monu- 
ment erected over his grave. Many a hero and many 
a philanthropist lies in a grave that is unmarked by a 
stone, while the craven and the unworthy, who have oc- 
cupied places of honor during life, have been glorified by 
a monument to conceal their infamy after death, as the 
glitter of state concealed it while living. There would 
be some wisdom in endeavoring to win a monument, if it 
were always the reward only of real merit and of virtu- 
ous actions. So pleasing is the idea of being honored by 
succeeding generations, that one who is striving through 
persecution, neglect, poverty, and disgrace, to accomplish 
some great good for his fellow men, might feel compensa- 
ted by the reflection that after death his actions would bo 
recorded with praise upon a conspicuous marble edifice. 
But those who lie under sumptuous monuments after 
their death, are the same tliat dwelt in mansions and pal- 
aces while tliey were living. These marble piles have 
too often been erected from the same motives that prompt 
the courtier to flatter those who are exalted by their po- 
sition ; and society has learned that the inscriptions upoif 
them, like the flattery of the living, are oflcn without 
truth. 

The simple headstone is seldom intended for anything 
more than an object by which the friends might Identify 
the spot where the remains of the dead repose. It is 
commonly a mere tribute of friendship, designed not to 
exaggerate the worth of the dead, but to pay a tender re- 
spect to his memory. It tells tliat the buried one had 
friends who wished to keep him in remembrance, and to 




QRAVE5 WITHOUT A BTONBi 



b^ar an afRictionatc tciitimony to Ida virtues. When, tliero* 
fore, we look upon a simple lieatUtono, w© feci »ensIblo 
of no attempt to exalt the dead by factitious honors : we 
feel that every good citlsecn ia worthy of tliis liumble 
tribute ; and the record of his name mid ngc, ijf tlio time 
of liiB death, and some .few important circumstances of 
his history, may interest not only \\m living tinends, but 
likewise the posterity of the departed, when t\w present 
generation has pa^tficd away. 

But when wc sec a gravo without a atone, wo know 
that the person buried iherc was either friendless, or 
that his friends were too indigent to pay tins last respect 
to his obsequies. To a person of susceptible feelings and 
lively imagination, a mere suf^gcstion is often better than 
a full account of the circumstnnccs of an event. W^hea 
we examine a grave that is marked by a Btone^ the in* 
scription commonly satisfies our curiosity in relation to 
the dead. The name, the age, the birth-placo, and per* 
haps the character of the occupant arc there recorded. 
We turn away satisfied, and read the next epitaph. Ai 
we proceed we arrive at a green hillock that stands alone 
and is unmarked by a stone. The rising of the ground 
denotes the character of the spot. It makes known that 
the remains of a hunmn being are there deposited, and 
the imagination is left to conjecture the cause of the 
neglect that has attended him in his sepulture. 

It may be the tomb of a i^tranger ; of one who was 
lost from his family and fricnds| who have never heard of 
liis deathi who are still seeking for him, and waiting his 
return. He may have been tlie only son of a widowed 
mother, who lives in a foreign land, and who still daily 
oiFers prayers to heaven for his safety and his restoration. 
She wonders at his long absence^ and dreams not that he 
lies here in a stranger^s grave, which can never be known 



216 MOUKT AUBURK. 

as his resting place, and where no human being can 
identify his remains. How many tears have been wasted 
that should have been poured as a sacred offering upon 
this green turf I How many conjectures have alternately 
. raised the hopes and depressed the spirits of this bereaved 
mother, which might have been tranquillized, had she but 
followed the remains of this unknown slumbercr to his 
final rest I 

Many are the dead who lie in these nameless graves, 
and many who died with a pang of deep sorrow in their 
hearts, when they thought that they were far from those 
^ho knew them, and that their place of burial could 
never be recognized. Some perhaps would be dishonored 
by their own history: but how many are they whose 
lives would awaken in our hearts the most tender emo- 
tions, the liveliest interest in their affecting adventures, 
and the deepest sympathy in their misfortunes. The ob- 
scurity in which these unknown tenants of the grave is in- 
volved, affixes a romantic interest to their biography. No 
envy is excited by a view of their lonely resting place, as 
when a sumptuous marble crowns the sepulchre of one 
whom we knew and who was undeserving of honor. It 
was with a view to this escape from the envy of the 
world, that the youthful poet wrote these lines : — - 

^ Thus unlamented, let me die, 
Steal from the world, and not a stone, 
Tell whore I lie.'* 

Even those who during their life-time are desirous of 
fiune and position, feel that a monumental pile, be it ever 
so unpretending, is calculated to excite the envy « of the 
world, and tliat aflor all tlieir struggles for distinction, a 
humble life, and an unambitious grave, are perhaps the 
B)9it sincerely honored.

To bo interesting; in the highest degree, a gmvc should 
bo alone, or where but few others rest. Our synipatliics 
are lost in a crowd, and a single object that is calculated 
to touch the heart, always most powerfully excites the 
imagination. Sometimes by the way-side, or on the edge 
of a solitary pasture, have we encountered one of these 
neglected graves. The poor mortal whoso remains lie 
there, must havo been friendless, or he died, pcrhops, of 
some disease that caused him in his last moments to bo 
.forsaken. How many sad tales of afHiction might bo

set apart from the rest of the dead I What a bitter satire 
on tlie inhumanity of man might bo dmwn from their 
history : of their virtues unnoticed and unrewarded in 
their humble poverty ; of their oflences unjustly revenged ; 
their humble wants unsupplied; their modest ambition 
treated with scorn ; tlieir last sickness unvisitcd by 
friends, and their death followed by an obscura and 
solitary burial I 

But the faith of the Christian informs him tliat the 
dead will not be judged by die honors which a mistaken 
world may have heaped upon them, or by the neglect to 
which it may have lell them. The crown of righteous- 
ness may glow with heavenly lustre from tlie brows of 
many whose mortal remains lie obscui*e| degraded and ' 
forgotten, in these nameless graves.

THE CATACOUBS OF ROME. 

Epitomiucd riox tub ATLAima Montult. 

The Roman Catacombs consist, for tlio most part, of a 
Bubterrancan labyrinth of passages, cut tlirougli tho soft 
volcanic rock of the Campagna, so narrow as rarely to 
admit of two persons walking abreast easily, but hero 
and there, on either side, opening into chambers of vary- 
ing sizo and form. The walls of tlio passages, through 
their whole extent, are lined with narrow excavations, 
ono abovo another, largo enough to admit of a body 
being placed in each ; and when they remain in their 
original condition, these excavations are closed in front 
by tiles, or by a slab of marble cemented to tho roi*k, 
and in most cases bearing an inscription. Frequently 
there are several stories connected with each other by 
sloping ways. 

There is no single circumstance, in relation to tho cat- 
acombs, of more striking character than their vast extent. 
About twenty difterent catacombs are now known, and 
are more or less ojkju, — and a year is now hardly likely 
to pass without die discovery of a new one ; for tho 
original number of underground cemeteries, as ascer- 
tained from tho early authorities, was nearly, if not 
quite, three times this number. It is but a very few 
years since tho entmnce to the famous catacomb of St. 
Callixtus, ono of the most interesting of all, was found 
by the Cavaliere do Rossi ; and it was only in tho Spring 
of 1855, that tho buried church and catacomb of St. 
Alexander, on tho Nomentan Way, were brought to light. 
Earthquakes, floods, and neglect, have obliterated the 
openings of many of their ancve\\\i c^\xw^\x:rv^^ — ^xA. ^^

HEART BURIAL. 

FbOX " COBOKICLIB OF TUB TOMBB.** 

Tii^ custom of burying the heart separately from tho 
rest of the body prevailed in the sixtqenth century, in 
the case of death at a distance from home. * Tho body 
was deposited in a grave in tlio place where the person 
died, and the heart was sent homo to tho friends of 
the deceased. Thus, in 1569, Sir Robert Peckham, 
dying at Rome, his body was buried at St. Gregory in 
that city, and his heart at Durham Church in England. 

Mr. Steele, an early writer, relates the following inci- 
dent in connection with the burial. ** As I came accident- 
ally into tho church, Sept. 25, 1711, a vault at tho east 
end of tho north isle being opened, into which I went, 
and found a small box of lead fashioned like a heart, but 
flat, being scarce two inches in thickness, with the lead 
sawdered, but the under part corroded ; the heart of Sir 
Robert Peckham discovered itself, wrapiwd within several 
cloths, and still smelling strong of the embalmment.*' On 
tho lid was his inscription.

REPUBLICAN BURIAL. 



It is not every American who reflects, when he is de- 
signing \k monument, for his own use, or for a friend's 
memorial, tliat we arc memliers of a ivpublic, and that 
tho costly and highly decorated monuments and sculpture, 
which may be seen in some of tho cemeteries of tho Old 
World, are not fit subjects for our imitation. However 
capable wo may bo of equalling, or even excelling tho 
models produced by foreign artists, and however abun- 
dant our wealth — a simple stone, to stand as the memo- 
rial of our life, and the index of our place of repose, 
with a few obituary lines, and some pleasing devices upon 
tho stone, is tho most befitting a citizen of a country 
where all arc politically equal. An honest private citizen 
of a republic is as worthy as a monarch or a nobleman, 
who is possessed of the same virtues, of a monument to 
hand down his name and his deeds to posterity. But 
kings and noblemen are few, and private citizens arc 
many. Tho former might for centuries indulgo their 
last© in monumental sculpture, and when they died, bo 
represented in marble effigy ; and ages might elapse be- 
fore their monuments would become inconveniently nu- 
merous. But were all tho wealthy citizens of a republic 
to indulgo themselves in tho same luxury, it would 
require but a few years to cover all our land with monu- 
ments, until thoso objects, which are intended to awaken 
a roverenco for tho dead, would becomo a mere inane 
and ludicrous exhibition of pride. 

Tlio names of our ancestors arc perpetuated on their 
liumblo headstones ; and it is delightful to wander in old 
graveyards and read tUc brl^f Uf^\»t^ ^l ^xsa^ xsw^xs^^

FENCES AND HEDGES. 



A 



In our modem cemeteries it is customary to erect an 
iron fence around the spot which is appropriated to a 
single family, thereby setting it apart fi*om the remainder 
of the ground. This appurtenance is plainly no orna- 
ment to the place, and the present necessity of it is not 
] very apparent, though after an example is set before the 
I public, it is not difficult to account fur the general imita- 
tion of it. When Fashion has sanctioned any practice, 
people will accommodate thcm3clves to it, without regard 
to its needfulness or convenience. The original purpose 
of the fence was undoubtedly to protect the monuments 
from injury. It is manifest that no such protection as they 
afford is at present requii*ed, because bi*utc animals are not 
allowed to run at liberty in the grounds, and if any in- 
jury was designed by men, the fence could not prevent it. 
The principal objection to fences is, that they cut up 
the cemetery into numerous divisions ; they dei>troy the 
unity and harmony of the grounds, and conceal the 
monuments and other objects which ought to bo exposed 
to view. It is impossible to construct fences of any sort 
that will not produce more or less of these effects, and it 
would be an improvement of the general appearance of 
Mount Auburn, if tliero were no fences at all except the

INTERIOR BEAUTIES OF MOUNT AUBURN. * 

In treating of tlio interior bcanties of Mount Au1)iim, 
I shall confine my remarks cliicfly to the natural scenery. 
Every visitor must notice that the /pounds are remark- 
able for a great and beautiful diversity of surface, as well 
as a largo variety of trees. Long before any one thought 
of it as a cemetery, it was a favorite resort of student.«« 
and others who sought it on account of its beautiful and 
romantic scenery. There are hundi*ed.s still living, who 
remember it as the scene of many a delightful ramble, 
alone and with company. It was no vulgar place of re- 
creation ; but a spot whither the thoughtful and studious 
fled for that needful rest from mental weariness, or for 
employing themselves in tjmso studies of nature which 
arc even better than entire repose from labor. 

Many a student has resorted to these grounds for his 
first botanical explorations, as they were the nearest in 
the vicinity of the Colleges, where there was any remark- 
able variety of native ])lants. In these protected dales, 
the spring flowers made their earliest appearangc ; and 
in the same places, under the shelter of the old oak-trees, 
lingered the latest flowers of autumn. The name which 
was given to the place, is associated with some of the 
most interesting poetie images, and ser>'ed to endear it 
still more to the readers and admirers of the most de- 
lightful poem in the language. Eveiy spot within the 
grounds became at length hallowed in their remembrance. 
Every stream had its goddess, and every fountain its 
Naiad. Every wooded hill seemed to bo the haunt of 
the Muse, and some rustic deity presided in every grove. 
There arc many ]>ersons who may regret the changes 
which have taken place. When any spot is thus cu-

HUMILITY IN ARCniTECTURE AND MONUMENTAL 
SCULPTURE. 

TuEiut must bo something particularly pleasing in tlic 
virtuo of humility, or it would not be so often affected 
by those who do not possess it. I believe the expression 
of this quality has never been regarded as one of the 
beauties of architecture, because this art, fwrn the earliest 
ages, has been used almost entirely as an instrument of 
ambition. Still it is an important quality in home archi- 
tecture and private monumental sculpture, and its merit, 
if not acknowledged by artists in these departments, is 
clearly recognized in the works of the painters. As 
I modesty is a virtue in the greatest as well as the least of 
men ; in like manner humility sets off the graces of every 
beautiful structure and every beautiful house, from a 
peasant's cottage to the mansion of a nobleman. The 
public has committed the error of regarding humility as 
^he opposite of gftindeur : whereas the t)pi>osite of gran- 
V^deur is littleness or meanness, and the opposite of humil- 
ity is ostentation. Two oppositestcannot be blended in 
harmony, but the combination of grandeur and humility 
produces effects which are beyond comparison greater 
than cither of these qualities alone could produce. 
/ Humility in arcliitectui*e is obtained by the careful 
avoidance of every appendage and every quality in the 
i style of a building that seems to indicate an attempt on 
the part of the owner to render himself cons])icuous. 
We love to sec in the style of a dwelling, the evidences, 
not only of the comforts and conveniences of the house, 
but also, so far as they can be made to appear, of certain 
/estimable traits of the owner or occupant ** I take care in 
my solitary rambles," says St Pierre, ** not to ask infor-

the ];uul«*niK*/* The style of the laii j2»ca|)o aiid of the

ing in the artificial ohjoets of a landscajie, we ieel no

with them. — ^ 

Of all sinister qualities pride is the most easily mani- 
fested and the most despicable, when exhibited in works 
of art ; for men hate, even while they profess to admire, 
everything tliat arbitrarily exalts others above themselves. 
We dislike, in the divss, manners and conversation of a 
man, any appearance that ]>lainly intunatcs liis con- 
sciousness of superiority. This remark is no disnjrrccable 
reflection upon human natui*e ; for it is not actual 8U]»c-

of it. AVe are led instinctively to feel assured that the

It might occur to the critical reader of tlicso remarks, 
that if tlie principles they maintain were fully carried 
out, all houses would be hovels. With equal justice it 
might be said, if objections were made to covering . the 
])erson with jewel ly, that one was in favor of restoring 
the primeval costume of fig-leaves. Thi prcvalent 
' rivalry in dress, in fine houses, and in sumptuous monu-

sonal qualities ; and it is something that will wear away 
with a better civilization. When that enlightened ei*a 
arrives, both the art of dressing, and the art of building, 
^will bo more of a science and less of a pantomime, than at 
the present day. In that era of better civilization, art 
will be exercised to increase our own pleasures and com- 
forts, and at tlie same time to confer an agreeable satis- 
faction uiK)n others. It is used, at the ; present day, 
chiefly for the puq)ose of advertising, or rather, of pub- 
lishing the evidence of wealth. Monumental stones 
wliicli should be designed only to commemorate the 
dead, are erected now to gratify the pride of the living. 
Such a desecration of the art of monumental sculpture 
will be ridiculed, like any other folly, when men have 
become wiser and less idolatrous. At that period of intel- 
lectual progress, humility will be acknowledged as one of 
the beauties of a house, or of any other structure that is 
designed for private or domestic purposes. This prin- 
cij)lo is now verj' generally felt, but not understood. 

Humility of expression is aided by anything that 
causes a structure to manifest less sumptuousness and 
cost than might be discovered by careful examination. 
A work that cost an immense sum of money may |>ossess 
this desirable quality, while in a very cheap work it nxo.^

wealth, without any talents or virtues to distinguish hiin

ON THE AFFLICTIONS OF LIFE. 

From Zimmcrmaxst. 

Who has not in the momont of convalescence, in tho 
hour of melancholy, or when si^iui'ntion or death has de- 
prived one of tlic intercourses of friencUhip, sought relief 
in the salutary shades of the countr}* I Happy is the 
Ix'ing who is sensiMc of the advantages of a religious 
retinMuent from the world, of a sarriMl tranquillity, in

spicuous, when we compare the monies of thought which 
employ the mind of a solitary* philosopher with those of 
a worldiv sensualist ; the tiresome and tumultuous life of 
tlie one with the soft tranquillity of the other ; when we

oppose tlic fcnr and horror that disturb the dcath-bcd of

pxit of thase pious souls who submit with resignation to

feel the importance of turning the eye inwanlly upon 
purselves, if we would bear the sufferings of life with 
dignity, and die pains of death with resignation. 

Retirement affords us the most incontestable advan- 
tages, under the greatest adversities of life. The con- 
valescent, the unfortunate, the disappointed, hero (ind 
equal relief; their tortured souls hero find a Imlm for tho 
deep and painful wounds they have receivetl, and soon

affliction would fly with horror from retirement, if its 
friendly shades did notaflTonl them that consolation which 
they arc unable to obtain in the resorts of fashion. Tho 
subtle vapor which sensuality and intoxication shed upon 
the objects that surround a state of health and happiness, 
entirely disap|)ear ; and all those channs which subsist 
rather in imagination than reality, lose their iM)wer. To 
the happy every object wears tho delightful colors of tho 
rose ; but to tho miscrablo all is blank and dreadful. 
Tho two conditions are equally in the extreme ; but they 
do not, in either case, discover the errors into which they 
are betrayed, until tho curtain drop ; when tho scene 
changes, and the illusion is dissi|iate<l. 

How 4Uihappy should we bo, if Divine Providence 
were to grant us everything we desire I Even under tho 
ver}" afflictions by which man believes his happiness to 
bq destroyed, heaven may propose something extraordi- 
nary in his favor. New circumstances excite now 
exertions. In solitude and tranquillity, if wo earnestly 
endeavor to conquer misfortune, the activity of life, 
which, until tho moment of advorsityi had been perhaps

DEATH OF THE AGED UAN. 
Dt Mm. SiaouRXKT. 

Who scans the fulness of a powerful mind, 

Which more than fourscore years hath held its course 

Among the living ? We, of yesterday. 

Tread not its halls, with ancient pictures decked, 

Still freshening 'neath the ministry of time, 

Nor haunt its secret cabinets of thought, 

Where shadowy jicople of a buried age 

Sit in communion. lie who died to-day, 

Was rich in imagery of other times. 

Ye might have asked him, and he would have told 

How step by step, his native place threw off 

Its rude colonial features, for the garb 

That cities wear ; — and how the cow-path changed 

To a thick i)eopled street, and the cold marsh 

To garden beauty.

HALLOWED GROUNDS. 

TiMBf the great limner of nature, who tints tho IiilU 
and plains with verchirc, and furnishes the rocks with

places. Scenes that in the early period of youth were

place among our distant recollections, clothed with a 
lavish beauty ; filled with the light of brighter skies, and 
vocal with sounds that are sweeter than music. The 
human soul, capable of a variety of affliction and of 
solace, finds in each something to alleviate its burden. 
In the bosom of these scenes, wo arc trans|>orted in imagi- 
nation back to this romantic stage in the journey of life ; 
the streams renew their ancient babbling, and return 
sweet responses to our silent invocations ; and in the 
gleaming objects of the landsca|>e, we behold tho ha^y 
pictures of memory resolved into bright realities. Every 
once familiar object opens a separate page in our histor}' ; 
and when we are absent from them, the book is closed, 
and all our retrospect is dark and mournful. 

Man alone is sensible to those enjoyments that spring 
from contemplating the scenes of i^ast sorrows. Naturo 
has given us this sentiment, which is allietl to that of 
divinity, to raise us above our mere physical being to the 
contemplation of sublimer themes. I lence there is always 
Bomo religious faith — though it may be unattended with 
rational assurance «- accompanying the sentiment of 
pleasure that springs from the remembrance of affliction. 
Tho benevolence of tho Deity has converted all our 
sorrows into so many sources of happiness, causing them, 
after their poignancy is gone, to reflect back ui)on our

THE TIDE OF TIME. 

As streams aiv ever flowing to the bay, 
Borne by mysterious force along their way 
To join the sea — we thus are moved along 
The tide of time, with all the living throng. 
The summer flowers^, tli* autumnal fruits decay; 
All things inhabiting this earth obey 
One signal doom. We pass from youth to age, 
Through many a pleasant, many a weary stage ; 
And as the winds, with pensive murmuring, 
Scatter tUe Wvca u^otv llvelc fluttering wing, —
